REM sleep occupies the greatest percentage of sleep in the individuals and species that sleep the most total hours.","paragraphIndex":20053,"printedPages":[287]},{"label":"According to the activation-synthesis hypothesis, dreams","explanation":"According to the activation-synthesis hypothesis, dreams are the brain’s attempts to make sense of the information reaching it, based mostly on haphazard input originating in the pons.","paragraphIndex":20054,"printedPages":[287]}],"status":"whole_book_source_structured_classroom_notes","basis":"uploaded_textbook_pdf_and_markdown","evidenceMethod":"textbook_module_summary","sourceFactCount":4,"editorialVerification":"source-aligned, individual paraphrase review pending"}],"classroomParagraphs":["During sleep, we rest our muscles and decrease metabolism.","Fluid drains from the brain and spinal cord into the subarachnoid space, removing potentially harmful proteins and waste products.","People who don’t get enough sleep react more severely than average to stressful events.","Inadequate sleep is a major cause of accidents by workers and poor performance by college students. Driving while sleep deprived is comparable to driving under the influence of alcohol.","Even one night of sleeplessness activates the immune system, as if you were ill.","Even if we agreed on what is the most important function of sleep for humans today, it might not be the function for which sleep originally evolved.","All of those are valuable functions, but the original purpose of computers was to do mathematical calculations.","Similarly, sleep probably started with a simple function to which evolution added others later. A likely hypothesis is that sleep’s original function, and still an important one, is to save energy.","Sleep conserves energy during the times when activity would be ineffective and possibly dangerous.","When NASA built the Rover spacecraft to explore Mars, they included a mechanism to make it “sleep” at night to conserve the batteries.","During sleep, a mammal’s body temperature decreases by 1° or 2° C, enough to save a noticeable amount of energy. Animals increase their sleep duration during food shortages, when energy conservation is especially important.","Hibernation provides a useful comparison with sleep because both greatly reduce activity and conserve energy, but they are not the same state. In hibernation, body temperature can fall close to the surrounding temperature while remaining high enough to prevent freezing; heart rate and brain activity decline markedly.","Reducing activity and metabolism helps an animal survive periods when obtaining food would be difficult or inefficient. This is the main reason the textbook compares hibernation with sleep.","During hibernation, neuron cell bodies can shrink and many synapses disappear. These connections regenerate when hibernation ends, showing that the reduced state involves reversible neural changes.","If one of the main functions of sleep is to decrease activity at times of relative inefficiency, we might expect to find little or no sleep in species that are equally effective at all times of day.","In contrast to the fish in normal waters, which average more than 13 hours of sleep per day, the populations in caves have only 2 to 4 hours of sleep per day without a circadian rhythm.","Many animals that live near the North or South Pole greatly decrease their sleep during summer, when the sun is constantly above the horizon.","Two elephant matriarchs, who take charge of protecting their troops, were observed to sleep two hours or less per night. To do so, they evolved the ability to sleep on one side of the brain at a time.","These large birds never land on the water, because their feathers would become waterlogged and prevent them from taking flight again.","EEG records indicate that they have periods of slow-wave sleep in one hemisphere at a time, and REM periods in both hemispheres. Exactly how a mother dolphin, elephant matriarch, great frigate bird, or migratory bird decreases its sleep need is unknown, but the fact that it is possible fits with the idea that sleep is largely a way to conserve energy, and that the other functions of sleep can be performed briefly.","Animals that need to be on the alert for predators get little sleep, whereas the predators themselves sleep easily.","In contrast, if you learn something and then go to sleep, even a nap, your memory solidifies and may become better than it was before sleep.","Another study found that a nap that included REM sleep enhanced performance on certain kinds of creative problem solving.","Both REM sleep and slow-wave sleep have been linked to strengthening of memories.","Researchers recorded activity in the hippocampus during learning, and then recorded from the same locations during sleep, using microelectrodes within cells for laboratory animals and electrodes on the scalp for humans. The results: Patterns that occurred during sleep resembled those that had occurred during learning, except that they were more rapid during sleep.","As the brain replays its experiences during sleep it forms new dendritic branches that strengthen the memories.","Knowledge storage depends on highly synchronized sharp wave ripples that transfer information from the hippocampus An important way for sleep to strengthen memory is by weeding out the less successful connections.","Research by Gina Poe and others has found that the brain conditions during sleep are ideal for pruning ineffective synapses and deleting unimportant memories.","On average, people spend about a third of their life asleep and about a fifth of sleep in REM, totaling about 600 hours of REM per year.","One way to approach this question is to compare the people or animals with more REM to those with less.","REM sleep is widespread in mammals and birds, indicating that it is part of our ancient evolutionary heritage.","As a rule, the species with the most total sleep hours also have the highest percentage of REM sleep. Cats spend up to 16 hours a day sleeping, much or most of it in REM sleep.","However, although memory consolidation does occur during REM, many people take antidepressant drugs that severely decrease REM sleep without causing memory problems.","During sleep, because they are shielded from the air, they deteriorate slightly. According to this view, REM is a way of arousing a sleeper just enough to shake the eyes back and forth, and the other manifestations of REM are just by-products.","This idea makes sense of the fact that REM occurs mostly toward the end of the night’s sleep, when the fluid behind the eyes would be the most stagnant.","Dreaming is difficult to investigate because a dream is known mainly through the person’s report afterward. Memories of dreams fade quickly, and much of what was experienced may never be reported. This limits how directly researchers can connect brain activity during sleep with the content of dreams.","Researchers cannot observe a dream’s contents directly. They must infer those contents from what a person reports, sometimes after considerable forgetting.","A common approach is to wake a participant and ask what they were experiencing immediately before awakening. Shortening the delay may reduce some forgetting, but it does not make the report a direct recording of the dream.","According to the activation-synthesis hypothesis, a dream represents the brain’s effort to make sense of sparse and distorted information.","Dreams begin with periodic bursts of spontaneous activity in the pons—the PGO waves previously described—that activate parts of the cortex.","The cortex combines this haphazard input with whatever other activity was already occurring and does its best to synthesize a story that makes sense of the information.","Consider how this theory handles a couple of common dreams.","Your brain in its partly aroused condition feels the vestibular sensation of your position and interprets it as flying or falling.","An interpretation based on the activation-synthesis theory is that during REM sleep (which accompanies most dreams), your motor cortex is inactive and your major postural muscles are virtually paralyzed. The main objection is that most dreams have no apparent connection to any current stimuli.","The neurocognitive model regards dreams as thinking that takes place under unusual conditions.","It emphasizes that dreams begin with spontaneous brain activity related to recent memories—just as most daytime thinking relates to recent memories.","Researchers used a PET scan to determine which brain areas increased or decreased their activity during REM, which is correlated with dreaming. Further, a PET scan yields a clear image only if the head remains motionless during data collection.","They also inserted a cannula (plastic tube) into each person’s arm so that they could inject radioactive chemicals during the night.","Because the researchers foresaw the difficulty of sleeping under these conditions (!), they had their participants stay awake the entire previous night. The upshot of all this is that the dreaming brain has little sensory input, leaving other brain areas free to generate images without constraints or interference.","Because the primary motor cortex is suppressed and the motor areas of the spinal cord are inhibited, arousal cannot lead to action. Activity is suppressed in the prefrontal cortex, which is important for working memory and logical thinking.","Meanwhile, activity is relatively high in part of the parietal cortex important for visuospatial perception.","The idea behind the neurocognitive approach is that arousal of parts of the cerebral cortex develops into a hallucinatory perception, with no sensory input to override it. A behavior can serve its function even when we do not fully understand that function.","One important function of sleep is to conserve energy at times when the individual would be less efficient. Animal species vary in their sleep per day depending on their feeding habits and how much danger they face while asleep.","In addition to saving energy, sleep serves other functions, including enhancement of memory. REM sleep occupies the greatest percentage of sleep in the individuals and species that sleep the most total hours.","According to the activation-synthesis hypothesis, dreams are the brain’s attempts to make sense of the information reaching it, based mostly on haphazard input originating in the pons.","Normal sleep does not require the extreme reduction in body temperature and physiology seen in deep hibernation. The comparison is useful for thinking about energy conservation, not for treating both conditions as identical.","The text goes on to compare the activation-synthesis hypothesis and the neurocognitive model. Homeostasis describes processes that keep internal conditions within useful ranges, often by correcting deviations. Allostasis emphasizes adjustments that anticipate or respond to changing demands rather than holding every value fixed at one number. Regulation involves both physiological reactions and behavior. Seeking shelter or changing clothes can be as important as shifting blood flow or producing heat."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m9-1-study-2","text":"Endothermy has a cost and a benefit"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Maintaining a relatively high body temperature requires substantial energy, but it allows enzymes, muscles, and neural processes to operate reliably across changing external temperatures. Different species balance energy expenditure and environmental dependence in different ways. Humans combine physiological temperature regulation with purposeful actions, so understanding thermoregulation demands more than memorizing the body's average temperature."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m9-1-study-3","text":"Brain and body mechanisms"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Temperature-sensitive signals influence hypothalamic and other central systems that alter sweating, shivering, peripheral blood flow, and motivated behavior. Terms are defined in the module on the page number indicated.","paragraphIndex":20042,"printedPages":[298]},{"label":"How it works","explanation":"They’re also presented in alphabetical order with definitions in the book’s Subject Index/Glossary at the end of this book.","paragraphIndex":20043,"printedPages":[298]}],"status":"whole_book_source_structured_classroom_notes","basis":"uploaded_textbook_pdf_and_markdown","evidenceMethod":"source_heading_facts","sourceFactCount":6,"editorialVerification":"source-aligned, individual paraphrase review pending","followUpExplanation":"Redundancy reduces your risk: If one mechanism fails, another mechanism comes to your rescue.","followUpParagraphIndex":20040}],"classroomParagraphs":["Cannon introduced the term homeostasis (HO-mee-oh-STAY-sis) to refer to biological processes that keep body variables within a fixed range.","When the temperature drops below the minimum, the thermostat triggers the furnace to provide heat.","When the temperature rises above the maximum, the thermostat turns on the air conditioner.","Homeostatic processes in animals trigger physiological and behavioral activities that keep variables within a set range. Sometimes the range is so narrow that we call it a set point, a single value that the body works to maintain.","Similar mechanisms maintain constant blood levels of water, oxygen, glucose, sodium chloride, protein, fat, and acidity.","Much of motivated behavior can be described as negative feedback: Something causes a disturbance, and behavior proceeds until it relieves the disturbance. When the air starts to warm up, a hiker increases thirst and decreases urine production by the kidneys, anticipating probable sweating and dehydration.","To describe these dynamic changes, researchers use the term allostasis (from the Greek roots meaning “variable” and “standing”), which means the way in which the body anticipates needs, avoiding errors rather than correcting them.","Most of it goes to basal metabolism, the energy used to maintain a constant body temperature while at rest.","For most people, maintaining body temperature uses about twice as much energy as all the muscle activity, brain activity, and everything else combined.","A synonym is poikilothermic, from Greek roots meaning “varied heat.” An ectothermic animal’s body temperature is nearly the same as the temperature of its environment.","A desert lizard moves between sunny areas, shady areas, and burrows to keep body temperature almost constant. However, behavioral methods do not achieve the same degree of constancy that mammals and birds have.","A few large fish, including sharks and tuna, maintain their core body temperature well above that of the surrounding water most of the time.","The tegu lizards of South America, about the size of a large rabbit, increase their metabolism during the mating season, raising their body temperature to sometimes 10°C above that of the environment. However, if the air is humid as well as hot, the moisture does not evaporate.","They risk dehydration, a dangerously high body temperature, heat exhaustion, and organ damage.","If its body temperature drops below the freezing point of water, ice crystals form.","Because water expands when it freezes, ice crystals would tear apart blood vessels and cell membranes, killing the animal.","Many amphibians and reptiles avoid that risk by burrowing or finding other sheltered locations.","However, some frogs, fish, and insects survive through winters in northern Canada where even the underground temperature approaches –40°C (which is also –40°F). Some insects and fish stock their blood with glycerol and other antifreeze chemicals at the start of the winter.","Wood frogs in Canada and Alaska actually do freeze, but they have mechanisms to reduce the damage.","They start by withdrawing most fluid from their organs and blood vessels and storing it in extracellular spaces. Therefore, ice crystals have room to expand when they form, without tearing blood vessels or cells.","Also, the frogs have chemicals that cause ice crystals to form gradually, not in chunks.","As mentioned, we spend about two-thirds of our total energy maintaining body temperature (basal metabolism).","Maintaining body temperature must provide an important advantage, or we would not have evolved these energy-expensive mechanisms.","Birds and mammals keep warm at all times and therefore stay constantly ready for vigorous activity, regardless of the air temperature. Second, and more importantly, beyond about 41°C (105°F), most proteins begin to break their bonds and lose their useful properties.","However, to do so, they need many extra chemical bonds to stabilize their proteins.","In short, our body temperature of 37°C is a trade-off between the advantages of high temperature for rapid movement and the disadvantages of high temperature for protein stability.","Birds lay eggs and sit on them, instead of developing them internally, because the birds’ internal temperature is too hot for an embryo. Similarly, in most male mammals, the scrotum hangs outside the body, because sperm production requires a cooler temperature than the rest of the body. (It’s not just for decoration.) A man who wears his undershorts too tight keeps his testes too close to the body, overheats them, and produces fewer healthy sperm cells.","Behavioral methods of temperature regulation, such as finding a cooler or warmer place, also depend on the hypothalamus.","Physiological regulation depends mainly on the anterior hypothalamus and the preoptic area, located just anterior to the anterior hypothalamus.","It is called preoptic because it is near the optic chiasm, where the optic nerves cross. Because of the close relationship between the preoptic area and the anterior hypothalamus, researchers often treat them as a single area, the preoptic area/anterior hypothalamus, or POA/AH.","The POA/AH receives input from temperature receptors in the skin and organs, and about 30 percent of the neurons in the POA/AH sense changes in their own temperature.","If either the skin or the hypothalamus is hot, an animal sweats or pants vigorously and seeks a cooler location. After damage to the POA/AH, mammals regulate body temperature mainly by the behavioral mechanisms that a lizard might use, such as seeking a warmer or colder location.","The body reacts by shivering and other processes that produce a fever..","In other words, fever is something the hypothalamus directs the body to produce.","People lacking the receptors for prostaglandins and histamines fail to develop a fever, even if they have pneumonia","Because newborn rabbits have an immature hypothalamus, they do not shiver in response to infections. If they are given a choice of environments, however, they select a spot warm enough to raise their body temperature","Fish and reptiles with an infection also choose a warm enough environment, if they can find one, to produce a feverish body temperature.","Again, the point is that fever is something the animal does to fight an infection. Other things being equal, developing a moderate fever increases an individual’s chance of surviving a bacterial infection.","However, a fever above about 39°C (103°F) in humans does more harm than good,","Your body has physiological mechanisms to maintain constant body temperature, including shivering, sweating, and changes in blood flow, but you also rely on behavioral mechanisms, such as finding a cooler or warmer place, changing clothing, and so forth.","Redundancy reduces your risk: If one mechanism fails, another mechanism comes to your rescue.","We shall see this theme again in the discussions of thirst and hunger.","and a fever above 41°C (106°F) is life-threatening. Osmotic thirst responds to changes in fluid concentration, while hypovolemic thirst follows losses of fluid volume; sodium-specific hunger is related but distinct."},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The brain and hormones coordinate water balance with drinking behavior. Pay attention to how the book differentiates water replacement from replacing both water and salts."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m9-2-study-1","text":"Thirst is not one single signal"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Fluid regulation protects blood volume and the concentration of dissolved substances. Osmotic thirst arises when body fluids become too concentrated, creating a need for water relative to solutes. Hypovolemic thirst arises when fluid volume is reduced, as can happen after losing blood or substantial fluid. The two conditions can overlap but rely on partly distinct detection and hormonal pathways. This distinction explains why thirst cannot be interpreted solely as a dry mouth."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m9-2-study-2","text":"Hormones and the kidneys"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Antidiuretic hormone (ADH, also called vasopressin) helps the kidneys conserve water by allowing more water to be reabsorbed. Other hormonal and neural mechanisms respond to changes in blood volume and pressure. The brain combines these signals with information about the mouth, stomach, and availability of water to organize drinking behavior. Physiological regulation and the decision to drink are connected but are not identical processes."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m9-2-study-3","text":"Why salt matters"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Losing extracellular fluid can also produce sodium-specific hunger, a motivation to obtain salt as well as water. Drinking plain water alone is not always the complete solution to restoring fluid balance. The precise motivational response depends on what kind of deficit the body detects. Compare a person who has consumed very salty food with a person who has lost both water and sodium: their physiological needs differ even if both report thirst."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m9-2-study-4","text":"A compact way to revise"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"For each form of thirst, answer four questions: What changed in body fluids? Humans alter their strategy depending on the availability of acceptable fluids.","paragraphIndex":20019,"bullets":[{"label":"Underlying mechanism","explanation":"An increase in the osmotic pressure of the blood draws water out of cells, causing osmotic thirst. Neurons in the OVLT and subfornical organ detect changes in osmotic pressure and send information to hypothalamic areas responsible for vasopressin secretion and for drinking. The circadian rhythm triggers increased vasopressin release just before bedtime, producing increased thirst at the moment and decreased urine production during the night. The result helps you get through the night without either drinking or urinating.","paragraphIndex":20020,"printedPages":[303]},{"label":"Additional explanation","explanation":"The OVLT decreases thirst after drinking, long before the ingested water reaches the cells that need it.","paragraphIndex":20021,"printedPages":[303]}],"status":"whole_book_source_structured_classroom_notes","basis":"uploaded_textbook_pdf_and_markdown","evidenceMethod":"textbook_module_summary","sourceFactCount":4,"editorialVerification":"source-aligned, individual paraphrase review pending"}],"classroomParagraphs":["Species differ in their strategies for maintaining water.","Beavers and other animals that live in rivers or lakes drink plenty of water, eat moist foods, and excrete dilute urine.","Gerbils and other desert animals can go through life without drinking at all. They gain water from their food, and they retain water by excreting dry feces and concentrated urine.","Unable to sweat, they avoid the heat of the day by burrowing under the ground.","Yellowbellied sea snakes of the Indian and Pacific oceans require fresh water to survive.","They get it by drinking rainwater that falls at the top of the ocean during the rainy season, and then they conserve that water during the dry season that can last more than half a year. blood vessels. (The term vasopressin comes from vas cular press ure.) The increased pressure helps compensate for the decreased blood volume.","The membrane surrounding a cell is almost a semipermeable membrane, because water flows across it freely and various solutes flow either slowly or not at all.","The result is a higher concentration of solutes outside the cells than inside, and the resulting osmotic pressure draws water from the cells into the extracellular fluid.","When these neurons detect increased osmotic pressure by noting their own loss of water, they trigger osmotic thirst, a drive for water that helps restore the normal state (Figure 9.9). In reaction to osmotic thirst, the lateral preoptic area and surrounding parts of the hypothalamus increase drinking, while the supraoptic nucleus and paraventricular nucleus (PVN) stimulate the posterior pituitary to release vasopressin.","The vasopressin also inhibits your need to urinate and therefore enables you to get through the night without becoming dehydrated.","The OVLT also receives input from the tongue, and each lick of water decreases thirst by a tiny amount, long before the blood could distribute the water to reduce osmotic pressure. Receptors in the circulation to the liver detect the osmotic concentration of the fluid, and if it is low, they signal the brain to decrease thirst.","Those areas have some cells that detect osmotic pressure, some that respond to blood volume, and a few that are sensitive to both.","Angiotensin II also triggers thirst, in conjunction with receptors that detect blood pressure in the large veins.","However, thirst caused by low blood volume, known as hypovolemic (HI-po-vo-LEE-mik) thirst, differs from osmotic thirst, because you need to restore lost salts and not just water. When angiotensin II reaches the brain, it stimulates neurons that release angiotensin II as a neuromodulator.","That is, neurons regulating hypovolemic thirst both respond to angiotensin II and release it.","Whereas an animal with osmotic thirst needs water, one with hypovolemic thirst cannot drink much pure water.","The animal therefore increases its preference for salty water. Deficiency in sodium provokes sodium-specific hunger, a drive to consume salty tastes.","When the body’s sodium reserves are low, the adrenal glands release the hormone aldosterone (al-DOSSter-one) that causes the kidneys, salivary glands, and sweat glands to retain salt. Therefore, he was constantly excreting sodium and he needed to ingest it constantly to replenish his supplies.","Either one by itself produces only a small increase in sodium intake, but their combined effect is substantial,.","Animals have evolved ways of maintaining body water, ranging from frequent drinking to extreme conservation of fluids. Humans alter their strategy depending on the availability of acceptable fluids.","An increase in the osmotic pressure of the blood draws water out of cells, causing osmotic thirst. Neurons in the OVLT and subfornical organ detect changes in osmotic pressure and send information to hypothalamic areas responsible for vasopressin secretion and for drinking. The circadian rhythm triggers increased vasopressin release just before bedtime, producing increased thirst at the moment and decreased urine production during the night. The result helps you get through the night without either drinking or urinating.","The OVLT decreases thirst after drinking, long before the ingested water reaches the cells that need it."],"classroomCoverage":{"status":"source_structured_full_book","level":"comprehensive_book_derived","topicHeadings":4,"bullets":15,"newWords":657,"allTopicsWithExplanations":true,"sourceScope":"verified_pdf_topic_facts_crosschecked_with_uploaded_markdown"},"classroomFigures":[{"id":"figure-9-8","headingId":"bp14-m9-2-h02","number":"9.8","title":"The Avoid treating hunger as a single on-off switch: oral, intestinal, metabolic, and neural factors all contribute."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m9-3-study-1","text":"Food choice and energy balance"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Hunger is influenced by energy needs, available foods, learned preferences, and sensory experience. Digestion converts ingested food into substances the body can use and changes hormonal and neural signals. The factors determining what someone chooses to eat are not identical to those determining when they stop eating. Biological regulation operates over short time scales within a meal and longer time scales reflecting stored energy and metabolic state."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m9-3-study-2","text":"Signals from the body"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The mouth and gut contribute to eating behavior through sensory feedback, distension, nutrient detection, and gastrointestinal signaling. Blood glucose is regulated with help from insulin and glucagon; disturbances in this system can alter appetite and health. Leptin, produced largely by fat tissue, provides information about stored energy over longer periods. No single hormone can be labeled the universal 'hunger chemical' because feeding is coordinated through multiple interacting signals."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m9-3-study-3","text":"Hypothalamic networks"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"The arcuate nucleus and paraventricular hypothalamus participate in circuits that respond to energy-related hormones and regulate feeding. Lateral and medial hypothalamic regions also influence eating, metabolism, and related behaviors. Early work sometimes described specific sites as hunger or satiety centers, but current understanding emphasizes distributed networks and several cell types. Damage or stimulation can change feeding without proving that one nucleus alone controls the whole behavior."},{"type":"heading","id":"bp14-m9-3-study-4","text":"Eating disorders and weight regulation"},{"type":"paragraph","text":"Genetic variation, environment, learning, and neural regulation all contribute to body weight and eating patterns. Bulimia nervosa and anorexia nervosa involve different characteristic difficulties and cannot be reduced to an ordinary desire to eat more or less. Weight-loss methods vary in effectiveness and can have physiological consequences. The course lesson is to avoid one-factor explanations: food behavior reflects the interaction of motivation, metabolic signals, cognition, and social
